The paper presents a potentially useful approach to crowd counting by combining visual and WiFi data using a Transformer-based architecture. However, the lack of novelty in the core ideas, the limited dataset and experimental validation, and the need for better justification of architectural choices and more robust comparisons prevent it from being a truly significant contribution.  While the performance improvements are promising, further research is needed to address the limitations and establish the generalizability and practical value of the approach.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates a potentially useful combination of existing techniques, but it suffers from limited novelty and experimental validation. Its significance is moderate, mainly due to its focus on a practical problem and its potential for further development. Without addressing the weaknesses described above, the paper does not represent a breakthrough contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 5/10

### Integration Flow Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20179v1)
- **Authors**: Jingjing Wang, Dan Zhang, Joshua Luo, Yin Yang, Feng Luo
- **Abstract**: Ordinary differential equation (ODE) based generative models have emerged as a powerful approach for producing high-quality samples in many applications. However, the ODE-based methods either suffer the discretization error of numerical solvers of ODE, which restricts the quality of samples when only a few NFEs are used, or struggle with training instability. In this paper, we proposed Integration Flow, which directly learns the integral of ODE-based trajectory paths without solving the ODE functions. Moreover, Integration Flow explicitly incorporates the target state $\mathbf{x}_0$ as the anchor state in guiding the reverse-time dynamics. We have theoretically proven this can contribute to both stability and accuracy. To the best of our knowledge, Integration Flow is the first model with a unified structure to estimate ODE-based generative models and the first to show the exact straightness of 1-Rectified Flow without reflow. Through theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations, we show that Integration Flows achieve improved performance when it is applied to existing ODE-based models, such as diffusion models, Rectified Flows, and PFGM++. Specifically, Integration Flow achieves one-step generation on CIFAR10 with FIDs of 2.86 for the Variance Exploding (VE) diffusion model, 3.36 for rectified flow without reflow, and 2.91 for PFGM++; and on ImageNet with FIDs of 4.09 for VE diffusion model, 4.35 for rectified flow without reflow and 4.15 for PFGM++.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Integration Flow," a novel approach to ODE-based generative modeling. Instead of iteratively solving ODE functions to map noise to data, Integration Flow directly learns the integral of ODE-based trajectory paths. The method explicitly incorporates the target data distribution as an anchor during the reverse-time dynamics to improve stability and accuracy, theoretically proven within the paper. Integration Flow provides a unified framework for existing ODE-based generative models like diffusion models, Rectified Flows, and PFGM++, leading to improved sample quality and efficiency by enabling one-step generation. The paper presents experimental results on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet, showing improved FID scores compared to existing methods, particularly with Rectified Flows and PFGM++.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of learning the integral of ODE trajectories rather than solving the ODE itself is a significant contribution. The framework's ability to unify different ODE-based models into a single structure and offer one-step generation is also highly innovative. While CMs attempt to do the same, they require special training which this model does not, and can be applied to more generative models. There is strong novelty when it comes to how the target state is explicitly incorporated as an anchor for improving stability.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses key limitations of ODE-based generative models, namely the discretization errors of numerical solvers and training instability. Improving sampling efficiency and enabling one-step generation is a significant step toward making these models more practical for real-world applications. If Integration Flow indeed generalizes well across different ODE-based models, it could become a foundational technique.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper includes theoretical justifications for the proposed method's stability and accuracy and an optimality proof. The equations seem well-defined, and the claims appear consistent with the presented analysis.
*   **Experimental Evaluation:** The experimental results are compelling, showing improved FID scores for one-step generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet when Integration Flow is applied to existing models. Although the paper compares the model with relevant SOTA generative models in the experimental section, there should be a more indepth quantitative and qualitative comparative analysis that proves their model is a substantial improvment than previous work.
*   **Limitations:** The performance on diffusion models (VE) is comparable but not a clear improvement. The authors acknowledge potential hyperparameter optimization opportunities. The paper can be improved by proving the improvement on VE.
*   **Clarity:** The paper can be difficult to follow for someone not already familiar with ODE-based generative modeling. Some aspects of the integration process could be clarified.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a compelling, novel approach to ODE-based generative models with supporting theoretical justifications. The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements in sample quality and generation efficiency. The key advantage is its potential to unify and accelerate different ODE models without complicated training strategies such as the VE models, which demonstrates it's flexibility.

However, the incremental improvements on VE diffusion may suggest that there is still room for development in particular hyperparameter optimizations. The paper also assumes familiarity with the area; a clearer exposition would enhance accessibility. Despite these points, the core concept is innovative and has the potential to considerably change the landscape of generative models.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### BLADE: Benchmark suite for LLM-driven Automated Design and Evolution of iterative optimisation heuristics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20183v1)
- **Authors**: Niki van Stein, Anna V. Kononova, Haoran Yin, Thomas Bäck
- **Abstract**: The application of Large Language Models (LLMs) for Automated Algorithm Discovery (AAD), particularly for optimisation heuristics, is an emerging field of research. This emergence necessitates robust, standardised benchmarking practices to rigorously evaluate the capabilities and limitations of LLM-driven AAD methods and the resulting generated algorithms, especially given the opacity of their design process and known issues with existing benchmarks. To address this need, we introduce BLADE (Benchmark suite for LLM-driven Automated Design and Evolution), a modular and extensible framework specifically designed for benchmarking LLM-driven AAD methods in a continuous black-box optimisation context. BLADE integrates collections of benchmark problems (including MA-BBOB and SBOX-COST among others) with instance generators and textual descriptions aimed at capability-focused testing, such as generalisation, specialisation and information exploitation. It offers flexible experimental setup options, standardised logging for reproducibility and fair comparison, incorporates methods for analysing the AAD process (e.g., Code Evolution Graphs and various visualisation approaches) and facilitates comparison against human-designed baselines through integration with established tools like IOHanalyser and IOHexplainer. BLADE provides an `out-of-the-box' solution to systematically evaluate LLM-driven AAD approaches. The framework is demonstrated through two distinct use cases exploring mutation prompt strategies and function specialisation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BLADE (Benchmark suite for LLM-driven Automated Design and Evolution), a new benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating Large Language Model (LLM)-driven Automated Algorithm Discovery (AAD) methods in continuous black-box optimization. BLADE provides a modular and extensible environment that integrates benchmark problems (including established suites like MA-BBOB and SBOX-COST, as well as real-world problems), LLMs, and evaluation metrics. It aims to address the limitations of existing benchmarks and to provide a standardized platform for comparing LLM-driven AAD methods against human-designed baselines. The framework offers functionalities such as instance generation, flexible experiment setups, standardized logging, AAD process analysis tools (e.g., Code Evolution Graphs), and integration with IOHanalyzer/IOHexplainer for performance comparison. The paper demonstrates BLADE's utility through two use cases: one focusing on comparing mutation prompt strategies and the other on function specialization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of a dedicated benchmarking suite, BLADE, tailored specifically for the emerging field of LLM-driven AAD. While individual components such as benchmark problems and evaluation metrics are not entirely new, their integration within a modular framework designed *specifically* for LLM-driven algorithm discovery provides a valuable and arguably necessary contribution. Existing benchmarking tools might be adaptable, but they are not purpose-built for the nuances of AAD, such as the need to analyze the evolution of code generated by LLMs or to control the specific information (e.g., textual descriptions) provided to the LLM. Moreover, the tool supports the analysis of code evolution graphs, which is a nice feature in the realm of LLM driven algorithm design.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the need for rigorous evaluation in the rapidly evolving field of LLM-driven AAD. The opacity of LLM-generated algorithms and the issues with existing benchmarks (e.g., generalization problems, lack of diversity in problem instances) make standardized benchmarking crucial. BLADE addresses these concerns by providing a comprehensive framework that allows researchers to systematically evaluate the capabilities and limitations of LLM-driven AAD methods. The inclusion of capability-focused benchmarks (testing generalization, specialization, and information exploitation) is a valuable feature. The integration with existing tools like IOHanalyser and IOHexplainer further enhances its usability and impact. The use cases, while somewhat limited, do demonstrate the framework's functionality. A strength of the paper is that it also clearly defines different "levels" of capability-focused benchmarking (e.g., looking for generalisation vs problem class specialisation).
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Modularity and Extensibility:** BLADE's modular design allows for easy adaptation and extension with new benchmark problems, LLMs, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Capability-Focused Benchmarks:** The focus on generalization, specialization, and information exploitation is a valuable addition to traditional performance benchmarking.
    *   **AAD Process Analysis Tools:** The inclusion of Code Evolution Graphs and other analysis tools provides insight into the LLM's design process.
    *   **Integration with Existing Tools:** The integration with IOHanalyser and IOHexplainer facilitates performance comparison against established baselines.
    *   **Transparency and Reproducibility:** Publicly available code and data contribute to transparency and reproducibility.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Use Cases:** The two use cases presented are relatively simple and could be expanded to demonstrate the full potential of BLADE on more complex problems.
    *   **LLM Selection**: The tool uses the Gemini flash, which may not be the best, given that many others LLMs exist for code design.

**Overall Assessment:**

BLADE represents a significant step forward in addressing the need for standardized and rigorous evaluation of LLM-driven AAD methods. Its modular design, capability-focused benchmarks, and integration with existing tools make it a valuable resource for researchers in this emerging field. While the use cases could be more extensive, the framework's overall potential impact is undeniable. The project is also open source, which further promotes its adoption and expansion by others in the field.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Prompting LLMs for Code Editing: Struggles and Remedies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20196v1)
- **Authors**: Daye Nam, Ahmed Omran, Ambar Murillo, Saksham Thakur, Abner Araujo, Marcel Blistein, Alexander Frömmgen, Vincent Hellendoorn, Satish Chandra
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming software engineering, with coding assistants embedded in an IDE becoming increasingly prevalent. While research has focused on improving the tools and understanding developer perceptions, a critical gap exists in understanding how developers actually use these tools in their daily workflows, and, crucially, where they struggle. This paper addresses part of this gap through a multi-phased investigation of developer interactions with an LLM-powered code editing and transformation feature, Transform Code, in an IDE widely used at Google. First, we analyze telemetry logs of the feature usage, revealing that frequent re-prompting can be an indicator of developer struggles with using Transform Code. Second, we conduct a qualitative analysis of unsatisfactory requests, identifying five key categories of information often missing from developer prompts. Finally, based on these findings, we propose and evaluate a tool, AutoPrompter, for automatically improving prompts by inferring missing information from the surrounding code context, leading to a 27% improvement in edit correctness on our test set.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper titled "Prompting LLMs for Code Editing: Struggles and Remedies" investigates the integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into software engineering tools, specifically focusing on developer interactions with a code editing feature called Transform Code within a widely used Integrated Development Environment (IDE) at Google. The study comprises three main components:  1. An analysis of telemetry logs that indicates frequent re-prompting as a sign of developer struggle when using the feature. 2. A qualitative assessment of unsatisfactory prompts, identifying five categories of information frequently absent that could enhance prompt effectiveness. 3. The development of a tool named AutoPrompter, which leverages contextual code information to automatically enhance prompts, resulting in a notable 27% improvement in edit correctness based on experimental evaluations. **Evaluation:** The novelty of this paper lies in its comprehensive examination of the practical usage of LLMs in code editing within real developer workflows. Unlike previous studies that have primarily focused on tool improvement or user perceptions, this research directly addresses the challenge developers face when using such tools and proposes a tangible solution. **Strengths:** - The dual approach of quantitative data analysis and qualitative insights provides a well-rounded understanding of the issues at play. - The identification of specific areas where prompts fail allows for targeted improvements, which is a significant contribution to the field of software engineering tools. - The introduction of AutoPrompter demonstrates a clear application of the findings, contributing to both theoretical understanding and practical enhancement of code editing tools. **Weaknesses:** - While the quantitative improvement of 27% in edit correctness is promising, the paper does not provide enough detail about how this measurement was obtained or validated, limiting the replicability of the results. - The scope of the study is somewhat confined to a single IDE within a specific organization (Google), which may affect the generalizability of the findings across different environments or for a broader developer audience. - More discussion surrounding the limitations of AutoPrompter and potential user feedback would enhance the understanding of how it performs in diverse contexts. Considering its insights into prompt effectiveness and concrete applications in tooling, this paper represents a meaningful step in understanding and improving the interaction between developers and LLMs in code editing. However, its limitations in scope and methodological transparency should be recognized. **Score: 7**  This score reflects a solid contribution to the field while noting that there are areas for improvement in study design and result communication, which could further strengthen the impact of the findings.
- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Large Language Models Learn Formal Logic? A Data-Driven Training and Evaluation Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20213v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Xia, Akanksha Atrey, Fadoua Khmaissia, Kedar S. Namjoshi
- **Abstract**: This paper investigates the logical reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). For a precisely defined yet tractable formulation, we choose the conceptually simple but technically complex task of constructing proofs in Boolean logic. A trained LLM receives as input a set of assumptions and a goal, and produces as output a proof that formally derives the goal from the assumptions. Incorrect proofs are caught by an automated proof checker. A critical obstacle for training is the scarcity of real-world proofs. We propose an efficient, randomized procedure for synthesizing valid proofs and introduce Template Transformation, a data augmentation technique that enhances the model's ability to handle complex logical expressions. The central evaluation question is whether an LLM has indeed learned to reason. We propose tests to measure the reasoning ability of a black-box LLM. By these measures, experiments demonstrate strong reasoning capabilities for assertions with short proofs, which decline with proof complexity. Notably, template transformation improves accuracy even for smaller models, suggesting its effectiveness across model scales.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the logical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) by focusing on the task of constructing proofs in Boolean logic. LLMs are trained to generate formal proofs given a set of assumptions and a goal. To address the scarcity of training data, the authors propose a randomized procedure for synthesizing valid proofs and introduce "Template Transformation," a data augmentation technique designed to improve the model's ability to handle complex logical expressions. The paper evaluates the reasoning ability of LLMs using metrics that assess semantic understanding over syntactic memorization, generalization to deeper proofs, handling wider expressions, and diversity in generated proofs. Experiments using Llama models demonstrate that fine-tuning with synthetic data and template transformations can lead to strong reasoning capabilities for short proofs, although performance declines with proof complexity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper introduces Template Transformation, a novel data augmentation technique for enhancing the ability of LLMs to understand the structural essence of proofs rather than specific token sequences. This is a tangible contribution, moving beyond mere scaling or naive data generation. The rigorous testing methodology, with its focus on semantic generalization, is a step forward. Also the choice of using Boolean Logic as the core problem is also something new, due to its complexity and formal rules.

*   **Weaknesses:** The synthetic data generation is not entirely novel in itself (though the specific randomization is). Also using a formal language to train and infer on a LLM it is also limiting when compared to the general purpose usage of this kind of models, focusing it into this specific problem.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper makes a clear case that LLMs *can* learn to reason formally, at least to a point. This challenges the notion that LLMs are only good at manipulating text. The exploration of the interaction between model size, training data size, and the template transformation technique is useful and suggests a path forward. It's also significant that a relatively small (8B) fine-tuned model outperformed larger models (GPT-4) in few-shot settings. Also given that the problem of proof solving is co-NP complete, there are multiple real-world applications for any model that performs well, not only as solvers but as verifiers.

*   **Weaknesses:** The scope is limited to Boolean logic and a relatively simple proof system (Hilbert). While justified by the desire for tractability and precise evaluation, it's a far cry from real-world mathematical reasoning. The sharp performance decline with proof complexity is a major limitation. The emphasis on achieving high performance can lead to the overlook of other important parameters, like the memory cost or the scalability. It remains unclear how well these techniques would transfer to more complex logics or tasks.

**Overall:**

The paper provides valuable insights into the formal reasoning abilities of LLMs and introduces a data augmentation technique that demonstrates promising results. The research shows real results, outperforming GPT4. It's well-structured, and the testing methodology is sound, if limited. However, the relatively narrow focus and the rapid decline in performance with complexity prevent a higher score. Also there are many factors not taken into account, like the memory usage of the inference or the scalability,

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Physics-Informed Diffusion Models for SAR Ship Wake Generation from Text Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20241v1)
- **Authors**: Kamirul Kamirul, Odysseas Pappas, Alin Achim
- **Abstract**: Detecting ship presence via wake signatures in SAR imagery is attracting considerable research interest, but limited annotated data availability poses significant challenges for supervised learning. Physics-based simulations are commonly used to address this data scarcity, although they are slow and constrain end-to-end learning. In this work, we explore a new direction for more efficient and end-to-end SAR ship wake simulation using a diffusion model trained on data generated by a physics-based simulator. The training dataset is built by pairing images produced by the simulator with text prompts derived from simulation parameters. Experimental result show that the model generates realistic Kelvin wake patterns and achieves significantly faster inference than the physics-based simulator. These results highlight the potential of diffusion models for fast and controllable wake image generation, opening new possibilities for end-to-end downstream tasks in maritime SAR analysis.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score with justification.

**Paper Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel approach to Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) ship wake simulation using text-conditioned latent diffusion models (LDMs).  Traditional physics-based simulators (PBS) are computationally expensive and difficult to integrate into end-to-end learning pipelines. This work trains an LDM on synthetic data generated by a PBS, paired with text prompts derived from the simulation parameters. The results demonstrate that the trained LDM can generate realistic Kelvin wake patterns significantly faster than the PBS while maintaining competitive visual quality. The authors argue that this approach offers a scalable and efficient alternative to traditional physics-based simulation for downstream tasks in maritime SAR analysis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:** The application of diffusion models to SAR ship wake generation conditioned on text prompts is a novel contribution. While diffusion models are well-established, their use in this specific context, especially for accelerating physics-based simulations, presents a new direction.
    *   **Speed Improvement:** The demonstrated speedup compared to the physics-based simulator is a significant advantage, addressing a major bottleneck in using such simulators. This makes the approach more practical for real-time or large-scale applications.
    *   **End-to-End Compatibility:** Diffusion models are inherently more amenable to end-to-end training and integration with downstream tasks compared to traditional PBS, which require manual parameter tuning and are often used as separate data generation steps.
    *   **Control:** Text prompts allow for fine-grained control over the characteristics of the generated wakes, opening up possibilities for creating targeted datasets for specific tasks or scenarios.
    *   **Well-written:** The paper is clearly written and well-structured, making the approach and results easy to understand.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on PBS:** The method is still reliant on a physics-based simulator to generate the training data.  The LDM essentially learns to mimic the PBS.  This raises questions about whether the LDM can generalize to scenarios not well represented in the PBS training data or outperform the PBS in scenarios where the PBS itself is accurate.
    *   **Visual Quality Concerns:** While the paper claims "competitive visual quality," the quantitative metrics and visual comparisons suggest that the LDM outputs are noticeably blurrier and contain more noise than the PBS outputs. The improvement of those metrics will be important to get widespread adoption.
    *   **Limited Generalization Analysis:** The paper doesn't thoroughly investigate the generalization capabilities of the LDM. It primarily focuses on matching the distribution of the training data.
    *   **Dataset Size:** The dataset size (3450 images) is relatively small for training diffusion models. This likely contributes to the visual quality issues and could limit the model's ability to capture the full complexity of SAR wake phenomena.
    *   **Lack of comparison with real data:** The models are only compared with the output of the simulator, and there isn't a direct comparison with real SAR data. This comparison is important to determine if the model is actually useful in real-world scenarios.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The potential impact of this work lies in enabling faster and more controllable generation of synthetic SAR ship wake data. This could be beneficial for:
        *   Training deep learning models for ship detection and wake analysis.
        *   Creating augmented datasets to improve the robustness of existing algorithms.
        *   Exploring different scenarios and environmental conditions in a simulated environment.
    *   The use of text prompts for controlling the generation process is a promising avenue for creating more targeted and diverse datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel approach with promising results, the dependence on the PBS for training data, visual quality limitations, and limited generalization analysis prevent it from being considered a truly groundbreaking contribution. The speedup and end-to-end compatibility are valuable improvements, but the overall impact is somewhat constrained by the aforementioned weaknesses. A score of 6 reflects the novelty of the approach and the potential for future development, while also acknowledging the limitations of the current work. Further research is needed to address the visual quality and generalization issues and to demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach in real-world scenarios.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Enhancing Systematic Reviews with Large Language Models: Using GPT-4 and Kimi
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20276v1)
- **Authors**: Dandan Chen Kaptur, Yue Huang, Xuejun Ryan Ji, Yanhui Guo, Bradley Kaptur
- **Abstract**: This research delved into GPT-4 and Kimi, two Large Language Models (LLMs), for systematic reviews. We evaluated their performance by comparing LLM-generated codes with human-generated codes from a peer-reviewed systematic review on assessment. Our findings suggested that the performance of LLMs fluctuates by data volume and question complexity for systematic reviews.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancing Systematic Reviews with Large Language Models: Using GPT-4 and Kimi."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of two Large Language Models (LLMs), GPT-4 and Kimi, to assist in systematic reviews. The researchers compared the performance of these LLMs in coding text from a peer-reviewed systematic review on technology-based assessments, using human-generated codes as a gold standard. The LLMs were tasked with answering yes/no questions based on 32 articles. The study varied the batch size (single, four, or eight articles) and used 11 different questions from the original review to assess the LLMs' performance under different conditions. The evaluation metrics included accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score. The study found that GPT-4 generally outperformed Kimi overall but that Kimi performed better with single articles, particularly in identifying true positives. However, neither model reached a level of performance suitable for unsupervised use in systematic reviews. The paper concludes that further research is needed to optimize LLM performance in this task, including exploring prompt engineering and other LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper explores the application of LLMs (GPT-4 and Kimi) to a specific task within systematic reviews – text coding. While using LLMs for information extraction or summarization is becoming more common, directly comparing two specific LLMs (especially including Kimi, which is less explored in Western literature) on a detailed coding task with varying batch sizes *adds a modest degree of novelty.* The experimental design, focusing on the impact of batch size and question complexity, is a thoughtful approach.

**Significance:** The potential impact on the field of systematic reviews and evidence synthesis is present, but modest. Systematic reviews are labor-intensive, and automating parts of the process could significantly improve efficiency. *The study's findings, however, are somewhat discouraging.* The models, while showing some promise, do not perform well enough to be used unsupervised. The paper highlights important limitations and challenges that need to be addressed before LLMs can be reliably used for text coding in systematic reviews. The finding about Kimi's better performance on single articles is intriguing and could guide future research. Furthermore, it is not clear from the paper that similar experiments have not been already carried out.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Research Question and Methodology:** The paper clearly defines its research question and uses a well-structured experimental design with multiple metrics and controls (e.g., replicating LLM coding multiple times).
*   **Comparison of Two LLMs:** Comparing GPT-4 to Kimi, which is less commonly studied in the West, provides a broader understanding of the landscape of available models for this task.
*   **Evaluation of Batch Size:** Investigating the impact of batch size is a valuable contribution, revealing that performance can vary depending on how the data is presented to the LLMs.
*   **Analysis of Question Complexity:** Though not a major focus, the paper also acknowledges and begins to explore the influence of question complexity on the models' performance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** While the study is well-designed, it is limited to a specific systematic review on technology-based assessments. It's unclear how well the findings would generalize to other topics or types of research.
*   **Modest Performance of LLMs:** The overall performance of the LLMs is somewhat disappointing, suggesting that significant improvements are needed before they can be reliably used for text coding in systematic reviews. There is not a good discussion about how to overcome such limitations.
*   **Lack of Deep Dive into Error Analysis:** The paper does not provide a deep analysis of the types of errors that the LLMs are making. Understanding the nature of the errors would be crucial for developing strategies to improve their performance. What specific things do the models miss or interpret incorrectly?
*   **No Prompt Engineering Exploration**: The paper acknowledges prompt engineering but does not undertake to explore prompt engineering to improve the LLM's performance.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some strengths in its methodology and comparison of models, the rather lackluster performance of the LLMs, the relatively narrow scope, and the limited discussion on error analysis and lack of prompt engineering exploration limits its overall impact.

The study points in the right direction by highlighting the potential and challenges of using LLMs in systematic reviews.
The paper contributes incremental knowledge to the field, but it is unlikely to have a transformative impact.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Generative Diffusion Models for Resource Allocation in Wireless Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20277v1)
- **Authors**: Yigit Berkay Uslu, Samar Hadou, Shirin Saeedi Bidokhti, Alejandro Ribeiro
- **Abstract**: This paper proposes a supervised training algorithm for learning stochastic resource allocation policies with generative diffusion models (GDMs). We formulate the allocation problem as the maximization of an ergodic utility function subject to ergodic Quality of Service (QoS) constraints. Given samples from a stochastic expert policy that yields a near-optimal solution to the problem, we train a GDM policy to imitate the expert and generate new samples from the optimal distribution. We achieve near-optimal performance through sequential execution of the generated samples. To enable generalization to a family of network configurations, we parameterize the backward diffusion process with a graph neural network (GNN) architecture. We present numerical results in a case study of power control in multi-user interference networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a supervised training approach using Generative Diffusion Models (GDMs) to learn stochastic resource allocation policies in wireless networks.  The problem is formulated as maximizing an ergodic utility function subject to QoS constraints. The GDM is trained to imitate a near-optimal stochastic expert policy, and a Graph Neural Network (GNN) parameterizes the backward diffusion process to enable generalization across various network topologies.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach in a power control case study within multi-user interference networks, showing that the trained GDM policy closely matches the expert policy across a family of wireless network configurations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in applying GDMs for *imitating stochastic policies* in wireless resource allocation.  Existing works typically focus on deterministic policies or use GMs for synthetic data generation or data augmentation rather than directly learning the optimal *distribution* over resource allocations. The use of a GNN to parameterize the reverse diffusion process to handle varying network topologies is also a contribution, allowing the learned policy to generalize. The distinction between solving for deterministic solutions vs solving for a _distribution_ is key to understanding the novelty here.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the realization that optimal solutions in many wireless resource allocation problems are inherently probabilistic. Representing and learning these stochastic policies can lead to better performance than purely deterministic approaches. This is especially relevant in multi-user information theory where time-sharing plays a critical role.  The demonstrated success in the power control case study suggests that the proposed framework can be a viable alternative to existing approaches. The GNN parameterization also promises better generalization, a perennial challenge in learning-based resource allocation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology.
    *   The problem formulation is well-motivated and relevant.
    *   The use of GDMs and GNNs is appropriate for the task.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the potential of the approach.
    *   The explicit mention of limitations and challenges adds credibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance gap, particularly at lower percentile rates, needs further investigation.  While the authors acknowledge it, more discussion on why it occurs and potential solutions would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper's performance is only compared to Full Power and WMMSE baselines. It would benefit from comparison with other state-of-the-art learning-based and classical methods. The benefit of GDM is clearly demonstrated but the quantitative advantage over other learning models (e.g., GANs, VAEs) is unclear.
    *   The computational complexity of training and deploying GDM-based policies could be a concern, which is not discussed in the paper. Is this approach more computationally expensive compared to existing techniques.
    *   The discussion of network topologies is limited. It is unclear what level of change in network topology is allowed while maintaining efficient GDM adaptation.

*   **Impact:** If further developed and validated, the method could impact how wireless resource allocation policies are designed and implemented, especially in complex network environments where stochastic solutions are essential. Learning how to approximate these stochastic distributions with reasonable computational cost could also improve the performance of more complex and modern techniques like cell-free massive MIMO networks.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel approach to wireless resource allocation by focusing on learning stochastic policies using GDMs.  The combination with GNNs for generalization is also valuable. However, the limited comparison with other learning-based baselines and the lack of discussion on computational complexity limit its overall significance at this stage. It is also difficult to apply and immediately impact the design of future wireless networks due to its limited analysis of network topologies.

### Image Interpolation with Score-based Riemannian Metrics of Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20288v1)
- **Authors**: Shinnosuke Saito, Takashi Matsubara
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models excel in content generation by implicitly learning the data manifold, yet they lack a practical method to leverage this manifold - unlike other deep generative models equipped with latent spaces. This paper introduces a novel framework that treats the data space of pre-trained diffusion models as a Riemannian manifold, with a metric derived from the score function. Experiments with MNIST and Stable Diffusion show that this geometry-aware approach yields image interpolations that are more realistic, less noisy, and more faithful to prompts than existing methods, demonstrating its potential for improved content generation and editing.
- **Summary**: **Summary:** The paper presents a novel framework that enhances image interpolation in pre-trained diffusion models by conceptualizing the data space as a Riemannian manifold. By employing a metric derived from the score function of the diffusion model, the authors achieve more realistic and faithful image interpolations compared to traditional methods. Results from experiments conducted on MNIST and Stable Diffusion demonstrate the effectiveness of this geometry-aware methodology in producing higher-quality images with reduced noise and improved adherence to given prompts, highlighting its implications for content generation and editing. **Critical Evaluation:** The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of image generation and interpolation by introducing a method that fills a gap in how diffusion models utilize their learned data manifold. Unlike other generative models with structured latent spaces, diffusion models have previously lacked a robust framework for leveraging their manifold, making this work particularly innovative. **Strengths:** 1. **Novelty**: The concept of treating the image space of diffusion models as a Riemannian manifold introduces a new perspective that could inspire various future research directions. This framework could potentially be adapted to other areas of generative modeling. 2. **Empirical Validation**: The experiments on both MNIST and Stable Diffusion offer concrete results that affirm the proposed methodology's effectiveness, providing a solid basis for claims made in the paper. 3. **Practical Impact**: The implications for content generation and editing are significant, as the ability to produce higher-quality interpolations can enhance user experience in applications using diffusion models. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Generalizability**: While the paper provides promising results on specific datasets, further investigation is required to determine how well this approach transfers to other complex datasets or tasks beyond image interpolation. The reliance on stabilized models may also limit broader applicability. 2. **Lack of Comprehensive Comparison**: While the authors claim superiority over existing methods, a more thorough comparative analysis with other state-of-the-art techniques would bolster the claims about improvements in realism and fidelity. 3. **Complexity**: The mathematical formulations may be challenging for practitioners who lack background knowledge in differential geometry or Riemannian methods, potentially limiting the framework's adoption. Overall, this paper presents a promising advancement in the manipulation of diffusion models and lays groundwork for future research in manifold-based approaches to generative modeling. However, further validation across a broader range of datasets and methods is essential to fully establish the robustness and versatility of the framework. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### "I've talked to ChatGPT about my issues last night.": Examining Mental Health Conversations with Large Language Models through Reddit Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20320v1)
- **Authors**: Kyuha Jung, Gyuho Lee, Yuanhui Huang, Yunan Chen
- **Abstract**: We investigate the role of large language models (LLMs) in supporting mental health by analyzing Reddit posts and comments about mental health conversations with ChatGPT. Our findings reveal that users value ChatGPT as a safe, non-judgmental space, often favoring it over human support due to its accessibility, availability, and knowledgeable responses. ChatGPT provides a range of support, including actionable advice, emotional support, and validation, while helping users better understand their mental states. Additionally, we found that ChatGPT offers innovative support for individuals facing mental health challenges, such as assistance in navigating difficult conversations, preparing for therapy sessions, and exploring therapeutic interventions. However, users also voiced potential risks, including the spread of incorrect health advice, ChatGPT's overly validating nature, and privacy concerns. We discuss the implications of LLMs as tools for mental health support in both everyday health and clinical therapy settings and suggest strategies to mitigate risks in LLM-powered interactions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary of the paper and a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary**

The paper "I've talked to ChatGPT about my issues last night:' Examining Mental Health Conversations with Large Language Models through Reddit Analysis" investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT for supporting mental health. The authors analyze Reddit conversations from the *r/ChatGPT* subreddit to understand how users engage with LLMs for mental health support.  The study identifies several themes: users see ChatGPT as a safe, accessible, and immediate source of support, providing validation, offering actionable advice, and assisting in reflecting on thoughts and emotions.  The paper also explores the potential risks, including inaccurate information, manipulation, and privacy concerns.  Finally, the paper suggests ways to mitigate the risks and highlights the potential for LLMs to complement traditional mental healthcare.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Strengths:**

*   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and rapidly evolving area – the intersection of LLMs and mental health. Given the increasing popularity of LLMs and the ongoing mental health crisis, understanding their potential and pitfalls is crucial.
*   **Empirical Grounding:**  The study uses a real-world dataset (Reddit conversations) to examine user experiences. This provides valuable insights into how people are actually using LLMs for mental health, rather than relying solely on theoretical assumptions.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study covers a range of user experiences, identifying both the benefits (accessibility, support, reflection) and risks (inaccurate information, potential for manipulation, privacy concerns) associated with LLMs in mental healthcare.
*   **Nuanced Discussion:**  The authors acknowledge the limitations of their methodology and datasets and are careful not to overstate the potential of LLMs while also highlighting their promise.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper offers valuable insights and suggestions for mitigating the risks of using LLMs in mental health contexts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The analysis is based on a single subreddit (*r/ChatGPT*), which might not be representative of all users interacting with LLMs for mental health support.  The users in that subreddit are likely to be more tech-savvy and aware of LLMs than the general population, introducing a potential bias.
*   **Self-Reported Data:** Reddit conversations are self-reported and can be subject to various biases, including social desirability bias and a lack of context.  It's difficult to verify the accuracy of users' statements or their actual mental health status.
*   **Superficiality of Analysis:** While the thematic analysis is informative, it is limited by the inherent characteristics of Reddit data and the amount of context included.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** While the paper acknowledges the ethical issues, it might benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the complex ethical considerations involved in using LLMs for mental health, including informed consent, data privacy, and the potential for bias and discrimination.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** The findings, while valuable, are not revolutionary. Much of the observed benefits and risks align with existing research on online support communities and conversational agents. The novelty lies primarily in applying these insights to the specific context of LLMs.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper contributes to the emerging body of research on LLMs and mental health by providing an empirical analysis of user experiences on Reddit. While existing studies have explored the potential of chatbots and AI in mental healthcare, this work focuses specifically on LLMs, which are more powerful and versatile than previous generations of conversational agents. The paper is relevant because it can inform the design of responsible and ethical LLM-based mental health interventions.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is relevant and provides a useful overview of the landscape but possesses limitations in generalizability. There's novelty but not a paradigm shift.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Picture is Worth a Thousand Prompts? Efficacy of Iterative Human-Driven Prompt Refinement in Image Regeneration Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20340v1)
- **Authors**: Khoi Trinh, Scott Seidenberger, Raveen Wijewickrama, Murtuza Jadliwala, Anindya Maiti
- **Abstract**: With AI-generated content becoming ubiquitous across the web, social media, and other digital platforms, it is vital to examine how such content are inspired and generated. The creation of AI-generated images often involves refining the input prompt iteratively to achieve desired visual outcomes. This study focuses on the relatively underexplored concept of image regeneration using AI, in which a human operator attempts to closely recreate a specific target image by iteratively refining their prompt. Image regeneration is distinct from normal image generation, which lacks any predefined visual reference. A separate challenge lies in determining whether existing image similarity metrics (ISMs) can provide reliable, objective feedback in iterative workflows, given that we do not fully understand if subjective human judgments of similarity align with these metrics. Consequently, we must first validate their alignment with human perception before assessing their potential as a feedback mechanism in the iterative prompt refinement process. To address these research gaps, we present a structured user study evaluating how iterative prompt refinement affects the similarity of regenerated images relative to their targets, while also examining whether ISMs capture the same improvements perceived by human observers. Our findings suggest that incremental prompt adjustments substantially improve alignment, verified through both subjective evaluations and quantitative measures, underscoring the broader potential of iterative workflows to enhance generative AI content creation across various application domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of iterative, human-driven prompt refinement on image regeneration using AI models. The study explores whether iteratively refining prompts improves the similarity between generated images and a target image.  It also examines if image similarity metrics (ISMs) align with human subjective judgments of similarity in this process. The authors conduct a user study where participants iteratively refine prompts to recreate target images, both with and without ISM feedback.  They analyze the generated images using ISMs and compare the ISM scores with human rankings of similarity. Their findings suggest that iterative prompt adjustments significantly improve alignment, verified through both subjective evaluations and quantitative measures. The authors also found evidence of diminishing returns after a certain number of iterations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of human-driven iterative prompt refinement in image regeneration, while conceptually straightforward, has been relatively under-explored from a formal experimental perspective. The paper's novelty lies primarily in its structured evaluation of this process, focusing on both subjective human ratings and objective ISM scores. While some prior work has examined AI-led prompt optimization and single-shot human prompt inference, this paper emphasizes the *iterative* nature of the human contribution and its impact on image quality as evaluated by humans and ISMs. It also directly addresses the critical question of ISM-human alignment.

*   **Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:
    *   It provides empirical evidence supporting the value of iterative human-in-the-loop processes for generative AI tasks.  This is crucial for understanding how humans and AI can best collaborate.
    *   It validates the use of certain ISMs (PS, CLIP variants) as reasonable proxies for human judgment in this specific context (image regeneration). While no automatic metric can perfectly capture human aesthetic preferences, the study provides a baseline for using ISMs to assist or evaluate human-driven creative workflows.
    *   It highlights the potential for diminishing returns in iterative prompt refinement, suggesting areas for improvement in guidance or feedback mechanisms within the workflow.  This could inform the design of more efficient and effective AI-assisted creative tools.
    * It underscores ethical considerations by alluding to deepfakes and misinformation, opening doors for studies on prompt manipulation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-designed user study with clear tasks and controlled conditions.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation using both subjective (human rankings) and objective (ISM scores) measures.
    *   Statistical analysis (mixed-effects models, ICC, chi-square tests) is appropriate and supports the claims.
    *   Clear articulation of research questions and hypotheses.
    *   The paper discusses limitations of the study, addressing sample size, prompt set and the feedback mechanism used.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited participant pool (20 university students) restricts generalizability to a broader population.
    *   The study uses a fixed number of iterations, a potential constraint on user performance and introduces some bias.
    *   The finding that simply providing ISM feedback had little effect on outcomes, indicating the need for improved and user-friendly feedback mechanisms in future researches
    *   Doesn't delve deeply into *why* certain ISMs align better with human perception (beyond citing existing literature on CNNs capturing higher-order structures). Exploring the features that contribute to alignment of different ISMs would be beneficial
    *   Limited to image regeneration; doesn't assess the generalizability to other generative AI tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of human-AI collaborative tools for generative AI, particularly by informing the selection and use of ISMs for feedback and evaluation. The findings regarding iterative improvement and diminishing returns can help optimize the workflow. Also the identification of human-centered workflows using generative AI can provide better training tools and guide novice users.

**Overall:** The paper presents a solid empirical investigation into a practically relevant topic. While the underlying concept is intuitive, the study provides valuable, quantitative evidence that confirms and expands our understanding of human-driven iterative prompt refinement. The limitations, particularly related to the participant pool, the feedback mechanism used and generalisability of outcomes slightly temper the overall impact.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates a solid contribution to the field of human-AI interaction, with well-designed methods and supported evidence. However, the limited scope and the need for broader investigations restrict the overall impact and result in the score given.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### MicarVLMoE: A Modern Gated Cross-Aligned Vision-Language Mixture of Experts Model for Medical Image Captioning and Report Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20343v1)
- **Authors**: Amaan Izhar, Nurul Japar, Norisma Idris, Ting Dang
- **Abstract**: Medical image reporting (MIR) aims to generate structured clinical descriptions from radiological images. Existing methods struggle with fine-grained feature extraction, multimodal alignment, and generalization across diverse imaging types, often relying on vanilla transformers and focusing primarily on chest X-rays. We propose MicarVLMoE, a vision-language mixture-of-experts model with gated cross-aligned fusion, designed to address these limitations. Our architecture includes: (i) a multiscale vision encoder (MSVE) for capturing anatomical details at varying resolutions, (ii) a multihead dual-branch latent attention (MDLA) module for vision-language alignment through latent bottleneck representations, and (iii) a modulated mixture-of-experts (MoE) decoder for adaptive expert specialization. We extend MIR to CT scans, retinal imaging, MRI scans, and gross pathology images, reporting state-of-the-art results on COVCTR, MMR, PGROSS, and ROCO datasets. Extensive experiments and ablations confirm improved clinical accuracy, cross-modal alignment, and model interpretability. Code is available at https://github.com/AI-14/micar-vl-moe.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MicarVLMoE, a novel vision-language mixture-of-experts model designed for medical image reporting (MIR). Addressing limitations of existing methods, MicarVLMoE integrates (i) a multi-scale vision encoder for capturing anatomical details across resolutions, (ii) a multi-head dual-branch latent attention module for improved vision-language alignment, and (iii) a modulated mixture-of-experts decoder for adaptive specialization.  The model's performance is evaluated on diverse imaging modalities (CT, retinal, MRI, gross pathology), showcasing state-of-the-art results on COVCTR, MMR, PGROSS, and ROCO datasets, and demonstrating enhanced clinical accuracy and interpretability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Architecture:** The MicarVLMoE architecture presents a significant improvement by introducing MoE to MIR, which allows for token-dependent expert selection and thereby achieves improved specialization. The dual-branch latent attention mechanism is also a valuable enhancement over conventional self-attention, contributing to a better understanding of contextual information between images and text.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper extends MIR beyond the common focus on chest X-rays, including CT scans, retinal images, MRIs, and gross pathology images. Evaluating the model on diverse datasets like COVCTR, MMR, PGROSS, and ROCO provides a more robust assessment of its capabilities and adaptability. This strengthens the paper's claim of versatility.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The reported state-of-the-art performance across multiple datasets is compelling. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of individual architectural components to the overall performance.
*   **Emphasis on Clinical Accuracy and Interpretability:** The RaTEScore evaluation metric specifically considers clinical accuracy, which is essential for MIR tasks. The visualization of MoE experts and attention maps facilitates model interpretability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalization Strategy:** The paper mentions training MicarVLMoE individually on each modality due to domain differences, but this approach limits the model's ability to generalize across different imaging types in a true zero-shot fashion. Ideally, a more unified training approach enabling generalization to unseen modalities could have been included.
*   **Computational Cost:** While MoE can improve efficiency, it can still be computationally expensive, especially when scaling the model. The paper doesn't deeply discuss the computational resource requirements and trade-offs.
*   **Lack of Comparative Analysis with Other MoE approaches**: The manuscript would benefit from a comparative analysis that provides quantitative measurements of how MicarVLMoE enhances model performance in comparison to other MoE approaches.
*   **Incremental Advances**: While the proposed approach combines useful methods like multi-scale feature extraction and latent attention, the specific combination and integration within the MoE context are not transformative. They represent incremental improvements on existing techniques.
*   **Clarity of novelty:** The paper doesn't explicitly and clearly articulate the novelties of the proposed MicarVLMoE architecture, particularly when these methods are combined, especially compared to existing literature.

**Significance:**

The paper significantly advances the field of medical image reporting by introducing a novel architectural approach that incorporates a mixture of experts, latent attention, and gated fusion. The comprehensive evaluation across diverse modalities establishes new benchmarks. The interpretability features also make the model more trustworthy and suitable for clinical applications. While the architectural innovations are primarily incremental, the combined approach and comprehensive evaluation make it a valuable contribution.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's significant contributions to the MIR field. The introduction of MoE and the dual-branch latent attention mechanism are valuable enhancements, resulting in state-of-the-art performance on diverse datasets. However, the lack of a clear generalization strategy, the incremental nature of the architectural innovations, and the absence of detailed computational cost analysis prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. Furthermore, comparing with other MoE approaches or discussing a joint training strategy that improves zero-shot performance across different imaging modalities would have increased the impact of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### CarbonCall: Sustainability-Aware Function Calling for Large Language Models on Edge Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20348v1)
- **Authors**: Varatheepan Paramanayakam, Andreas Karatzas, Iraklis Anagnostopoulos, Dimitrios Stamoulis
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) enable real-time function calling in edge AI systems but introduce significant computational overhead, leading to high power consumption and carbon emissions. Existing methods optimize for performance while neglecting sustainability, making them inefficient for energy-constrained environments. We introduce CarbonCall, a sustainability-aware function-calling framework that integrates dynamic tool selection, carbon-aware execution, and quantized LLM adaptation. CarbonCall adjusts power thresholds based on real-time carbon intensity forecasts and switches between model variants to sustain high tokens-per-second throughput under power constraints. Experiments on an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin show that CarbonCall reduces carbon emissions by up to 52%, power consumption by 30%, and execution time by 30%, while maintaining high efficiency.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper titled **"CarbonCall: Sustainability-Aware Function Calling for Large Language Models on Edge Devices"** addresses the critical issue of high power consumption and carbon emissions associated with the deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) on edge devices. The authors present CarbonCall, a novel framework designed to optimize function calling by integrating several advanced strategies such as dynamic tool selection, carbon-aware execution, and quantized LLM adaptation. The framework adapts to real-time carbon intensity forecasts to manage power consumption effectively while allowing the selection between various model variants. Experimental results with the NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin demonstrate that CarbonCall significantly reduces carbon emissions by up to 52%, lowers power consumption by 30%, and decreases execution time by 30%, achieving a balance between sustainability and performance. ### Critical Evaluation **Novelty:** The concept of integrating sustainability into function calling for LLMs, particularly on resource-constrained edge devices, is relatively novel. While prior research primarily focused on optimizing performance, CarbonCall stands out by explicitly addressing the environmental impact. By incorporating real-time carbon intensity data into execution strategies, the paper ventures into a crucial and underexplored area of AI deployment where ethical computing is becoming increasingly important. **Significance:** The significance of this research lies in its timing and relevance as policymakers and tech companies increasingly target carbon neutrality. The results suggest that it is possible to reconcile high-performance AI operations with lower environmental impact, contributing to both academic discussions and practical applications in the field of AI and sustainability. **Strengths:** 1. **Innovative Approach:** CarbonCall's integration of sustainability metrics into AI performance metrics is a progressive step forward and encourages other researchers in the field to consider environmental factors. 2. **Robust Experimental Evidence:** The paper includes substantial empirical evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed method. 3. **Applicability:** As edge devices become more prevalent in various applications, the adaptability of CarbonCall can serve a wide range of users—from researchers to industry professionals. **Weaknesses:** 1. **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** While the paper showcases substantial reductions in power consumption and emissions, the experiments are confined to a single platform (NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin). Broader evaluations across different devices and real-world scenarios would strengthen the work. 2. **Generalization of Results:** The carbon intensity forecasts and execution strategies may not be universally applicable to all edge environments, which could limit the generalizability of the framework's effectiveness. 3. **Cost-Benefit Analysis:** The paper lacks a comprehensive analysis of the cost versus the benefits of implementing CarbonCall in terms of infrastructure and operational changes needed by organizations. Given the above considerations, particularly regarding its innovative approach and relevance to current global sustainability challenges, while also noting the limitations in scope and generalization, I assign a score reflecting a balanced view of the paper's contributions. **Score: 8**
- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 8/10

### Local Prompt Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20355v1)
- **Authors**: Yash Jain, Vishal Chowdhary
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the use of prompts to guide the output of Large Language Models have increased dramatically. However, even the best of experts struggle to choose the correct words to stitch up a prompt for the desired task. To solve this, LLM driven prompt optimization emerged as an important problem. Existing prompt optimization methods optimize a prompt globally, where in all the prompt tokens have to be optimized over a large vocabulary while solving a complex task. The large optimization space (tokens) leads to insufficient guidance for a better prompt. In this work, we introduce Local Prompt Optimization (LPO) that integrates with any general automatic prompt engineering method. We identify the optimization tokens in a prompt and nudge the LLM to focus only on those tokens in its optimization step. We observe remarkable performance improvements on Math Reasoning (GSM8k and MultiArith) and BIG-bench Hard benchmarks across various automatic prompt engineering methods. Further, we show that LPO converges to the optimal prompt faster than global methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Local Prompt Optimization" (LPO), a method for improving prompts for Large Language Models (LLMs). LPO focuses optimization efforts on specific tokens within a prompt that are deemed most responsible for incorrect predictions, rather than optimizing the entire prompt globally. The authors integrate LPO into existing automatic prompt engineering methods (APE, APO, PE2) and demonstrate performance improvements on mathematical reasoning (GSM8k, MultiArith), and BIG-bench Hard benchmarks.  The authors claim LPO converges to optimal prompts faster than global methods and allows for better control over which parts of a prompt are edited. The method also showed improvements on a production classification prompt.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of LPO – focusing optimization on specific tokens instead of the entire prompt – possesses a moderate degree of novelty.  While the concept of "local" optimization isn't entirely new in machine learning, its application to prompt engineering, explicitly identifying key tokens for editing, represents a valuable adaptation. The use of `<edit>` tags to explicitly mark the optimization region is also a practical contribution. However, the paper needs to acknowledge and discuss prior work in related areas, such as attention mechanisms or input-specific gradient analysis, where the concept of focusing on certain parts of the input already exists. Without this discussion, the claimed novelty is weakened.
*   **Significance:** The significance is promising, though not groundbreaking.  The reported improvements in performance and faster convergence are valuable for practitioners using prompt engineering. The ability to control which parts of the prompt are being optimized is also significant for production environments where specific sections of a prompt may need adjustment without affecting others. The paper's results on BBH are impressive in terms of diversity. However, the absolute improvements on GSM8k and MultiArith are fairly modest. A crucial aspect is whether LPO actually *generalizes* well. While the authors test across several tasks, the prompt identification method relies on the LLM itself. There is a risk of the LLM simply reinforcing its existing biases, leading to improvements on the training set but not necessarily on unseen examples. More robust validation, including out-of-domain evaluations, is needed to strengthen this claim. Also, the dependence on `gpt-4o` for identifying the tokens might hinder reproducibility as that is a closed source model.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies a limitation of global prompt optimization.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** LPO is presented in a clear and understandable manner.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper includes experiments on diverse benchmarks and existing automatic prompt engineering methods.
    *   **Practical Benefits:** The claims of faster convergence and control over prompt editing have practical implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty Argument:** The novelty isn't fully established due to lack of comparison with prior work in related areas (attention, input-specific gradients).
    *   **Dependence on LLM for Identifying Tokens:** The use of an LLM to identify the tokens for optimization raises concerns about bias and potential overfitting.  More rigorous generalization testing is needed.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While statistically significant, the performance gains on some datasets are not dramatically large, and could be explained through the increased beam size when using LPO.
    *   **Generalization Discussion:** The paper would benefit from a discussion about how the selected tokens generalize to new prompts, or when it might be useful to start from scratch.

* **Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 6. The paper presents a worthwhile, practical modification to existing prompt optimization techniques. The concept of local optimization is sensible, and the initial results are encouraging, particularly the faster convergence and control over prompt editing. However, the paper's novelty is not fully established due to a lack of acknowledgement of related fields. Furthermore, the potential for overfitting and the dependence on potentially biased LLM for identifying tokens are significant concerns that require further investigation. A stronger argument about the generalization capabilities and a more robust validation approach would raise the score significantly.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### DMDTEval: An Evaluation and Analysis of LLMs on Disambiguation in Multi-domain Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20371v1)
- **Authors**: Zhibo Man, Yuanmeng Chen, Yujie Zhang, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu
- **Abstract**: Currently, Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable results in machine translation. However, their performance in multi-domain translation (MDT) is less satisfactory; the meanings of words can vary across different domains, highlighting the significant ambiguity inherent in MDT. Therefore, evaluating the disambiguation ability of LLMs in MDT remains an open problem. To this end, we present an evaluation and analysis of LLMs on disambiguation in multi-domain translation (DMDTEval), our systematic evaluation framework consisting of three critical aspects: (1) we construct a translation test set with multi-domain ambiguous word annotation, (2) we curate a diverse set of disambiguation prompting templates, and (3) we design precise disambiguation metrics, and study the efficacy of various prompting strategies on multiple state-of-the-art LLMs. Our extensive experiments reveal a number of crucial findings that we believe will pave the way and also facilitate further research in the critical area of improving the disambiguation of LLMs.
- **Summary**: ### Summary of the Paper The paper titled **"DMDTEval: An Evaluation and Analysis of LLMs on Disambiguation in Multi-domain Translation"** addresses the inadequate performance of large language models (LLMs) in multi-domain translation (MDT), primarily due to the ambiguous meanings of words that vary across different domains. The authors propose a structured evaluation framework, DMDTEval, focusing on assessing LLMs’ disambiguation skills. This framework comprises three main components:  1. A translation test set annotated with multi-domain ambiguous words. 2. A collection of diverse disambiguation prompting templates. 3. Well-defined disambiguation metrics to assess the efficacy of various prompting strategies across multiple leading LLMs. The authors conduct extensive experiments that yield significant insights aimed at enhancing the disambiguation capabilities of LLMs, thereby paving the way for future research in this crucial aspect of machine translation. ### Critical Evaluation #### Strengths: 1. **Novel Framework**: The introduction of a systematic evaluation framework specifically targeting disambiguation in multi-domain translation is a notable contribution to the field. It fills a gap in existing evaluation methodologies that often overlook domain-specific ambiguities. 2. **Comprehensive Approach**: The three-pronged strategy involving dataset construction, diverse prompts, and precise metrics demonstrates a methodical approach to solving the identified problem. This can serve as a valuable resource for future researchers. 3. **Relevance**: Given the increasing use of LLMs in machine translation across various domains, enhancing their disambiguation capabilities is of critical importance. The paper addresses a pressing challenge, thus holding significant relevance in current research.  #### Weaknesses: 1. **Limited Scope of Evaluation**: While the evaluation is systematic, the paper could benefit from discussing how well the proposed methods generalize across various languages and domains beyond those tested. The results may not be universally applicable. 2. **Implementation Details**: More elaboration on how the prompting templates were curated and the rationale for chosen metrics could have strengthened the evaluation. Transparency in methodology helps in reproducing and validating results. 3. **Potential Biases**: There is a risk of inherent biases in the test set due to the subjective nature of ambiguous words. The paper could strengthen its claims by providing more insight into how they addressed these concerns during annotation. ### Overall Assessment The paper presents an important contribution that addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs within the context of multi-domain translation. Although it excels in framing the problem, proposing a novel evaluation framework, and detailing extensive experiments, it could be improved with additional robustness in generalization discussions and methodological transparency. Taking all factors into account, I would assign this paper a **Score: 7**. It represents a meaningful step forward in the field, with practical implications for future improvements in LLM disambiguation strategies; however, its limitations in scope and implementation details prevent it from being deemed exceptional.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Inception: Jailbreak the Memory Mechanism of Text-to-Image Generation Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2504.20376v1)
- **Authors**: Shiqian Zhao, Jiayang Liu, Yiming Li, Runyi Hu, Xiaojun Jia, Wenshu Fan, Xinfeng Li, Jie Zhang, Wei Dong, Tianwei Zhang, Luu Anh Tuan
- **Abstract**: Currently, the memory mechanism has been widely and successfully exploited in online text-to-image (T2I) generation systems ($e.g.$, DALL$\cdot$E 3) for alleviating the growing tokenization burden and capturing key information in multi-turn interactions. Despite its practicality, its security analyses have fallen far behind. In this paper, we reveal that this mechanism exacerbates the risk of jailbreak attacks. Different from previous attacks that fuse the unsafe target prompt into one ultimate adversarial prompt, which can be easily detected or may generate non-unsafe images due to under- or over-optimization, we propose Inception, the first multi-turn jailbreak attack against the memory mechanism in real-world text-to-image generation systems. Inception embeds the malice at the inception of the chat session turn by turn, leveraging the mechanism that T2I generation systems retrieve key information in their memory. Specifically, Inception mainly consists of two modules. It first segments the unsafe prompt into chunks, which are subsequently fed to the system in multiple turns, serving as pseudo-gradients for directive optimization. Specifically, we develop a series of segmentation policies that ensure the images generated are semantically consistent with the target prompt. Secondly, after segmentation, to overcome the challenge of the inseparability of minimum unsafe words, we propose recursion, a strategy that makes minimum unsafe words subdivisible.
